I mean… not like a passing worry where you think, “Okay, I’m stressed, I’ll deal with it.” It hits me hard, and it stays. It’s like once that thought shows up, everything in me reacts to it like it’s true.\n\nAnd the thought is basically what I said when I came in — if all my years doing electrical work, all that training and experience and being careful, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then how am I supposed to believe I’m not a danger to other people? To somebody working next to me, or somebody living in the same house as me? It’s not just an abstract fear. It feels personal, like it says something about who I am.\n\nI don’t want the thought there. I hate it. But I also can’t say it feels like some random thing that doesn’t belong to me, because part of me believes it. That’s what makes it so hard. It feels like a natural worry in the sense that it comes from something real, but it’s also… it takes over. It’s like my mind keeps using it as proof that I can’t trust myself, and I can’t get enough distance from it to just say, “That’s ridiculous.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever lead you to take specific actions or make decisions—like avoiding certain tasks, withdrawing from others, or constantly seeking reassurance—to try to reduce the risk or the anxiety you feel?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I don’t know if I always think of it as “reducing anxiety” in the moment, because I’ll tell myself I’m just being practical, or I’m tired, or I don’t have time for something. But if I’m honest, yeah, I avoid things.\n\nThe biggest one is my garage. Which sounds stupid, because it’s my own house, you know? But I don’t go in there unless I absolutely have to. If I need a tool or something, I’ll kind of plan it out, go in, grab what I need, and get back out. I don’t just spend time out there anymore. I used to be able to work on things, mess around, organize stuff, whatever. Now it feels like as soon as I’m in there, my mind starts going places I don’t want it to go, and I just… I don’t want to be in that space.\n\nAnd I quit helping with the electrical stuff at church. I used to be the guy they’d ask—outlets, lights, little repairs, checking something that wasn’t working right. And I just stopped. I made excuses at first, like I was busy with work or I had too much going on, but really I don’t want that responsibility anymore. I don’t want to be the person someone trusts with something like that, because then if anything goes wrong, it’s on me. Even if it’s small, even if I know how to do it, I just don’t want to be in that position.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about car projects, or electrical work on cars, or anything around the fire, I shut it down pretty quick. I’ll change the subject or leave the room or say I don’t want to talk about it. Sometimes I probably come off harsh, but I can’t sit there and listen to people pick through it or talk casually about that kind of thing. It gets under my skin fast, and I feel like I have to stop it before it goes too far.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I do make decisions around it. I avoid places, I stopped doing things I used to do, and I keep people from talking about certain things around me. At the time it feels like I’m just trying to keep myself steady, but it’s definitely changed what I’ll let myself be around.\n\nDoctor: Are these kinds of thoughts something you've been dealing with even outside of situations related to your work or your brother's accident—like at other times when you're not actively working on electrical things or reminded of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only when I’m standing in front of a panel or holding a tool. That’s kind of the problem. It started from that, from my brother and from the work, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box.\n\nI can be at home doing something completely normal, and the thought is still there in the background — that if all my years as an electrician, all the training and habits and double-checking, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m dangerous just by being the person responsible for things. Not just on a job site, but under my own roof too.\n\nIt’ll come up when I’m around my family, or even just sitting in the house. I’ll think, “What if I miss something here? What if something I did, or didn’t do, hurts somebody?” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I can’t fully trust myself around people who depend on me. So yeah, it’s connected to the accident, but it follows me outside of the obvious reminders. It’s more like a belief I keep carrying around, not just a thought that shows up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having similar kinds of thoughts or worries about being responsible for harm in other areas of your life, not just related to electrical work or your brother’s accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it started with the electrical work and my brother, obviously, but it spread out from there. It’s not like my brain keeps it limited to, “Don’t touch wires” or “Don’t take on a job.” It’s more like… if I couldn’t keep him safe after all those years of knowing what I was doing, then what else am I missing?\n\nThat’s the thought I can’t shake. I came in because I can’t get away from this idea that if my training, my experience, all the caution I used to pride myself on, still wasn’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. And that’s not just about a circuit or a tool. That gets into everything. Like being around people, being responsible for them, having them trust me.\n\nI know that might sound broad, but that’s how it feels. It’s like the accident became proof in my head that I can’t trust my own judgment. So even in other parts of my life, the worry is still basically the same: what if someone gets hurt because I was the one there, the one responsible, the one who should’ve known better?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a bad or worthless person, even when you’re not thinking about the accident or your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not even like I have to be actively thinking about the accident for it to be there. It’s more like this… weight that just sits in me all day. Guilt, mostly, but not just guilt. Anger too. And then I feel guilty for being angry, so it kind of folds back in on itself.\n\nI don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” in those exact words all the time, but the feeling is there. Like I’m the person everybody got left with. Especially with my nephew. When I look at him, I can’t help thinking, he lost his dad and somehow he got stuck with me instead. And I know that sounds harsh, and I don’t want to put that on him — I don’t say that to him or anything — but inside, that’s where my mind goes. Like I’m this poor substitute. Like I’m not the one who should still be here trying to be useful.\n\nAnd it’s not just one quick thought and then it passes. It sits in my chest, this heavy, tight feeling, and I carry it into regular moments. Eating dinner, driving somewhere, seeing him do something normal. I’ll look at him and feel this mix of sadness and anger and shame, like I failed him before he even had a say in it. So yeah… even when I’m not actively replaying the accident or thinking about work, I still feel like there’s something bad about me. Like I’m not enough, and maybe I never will be.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time, or have you lost interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the time” is hard for me to judge, because I’m not walking around every minute thinking, “I’m depressed,” exactly. It’s more like I notice I’m heavier than I used to be, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if that counts the way you’re asking it.\n\nAnd interest… I mean, I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of what I enjoy versus what I’m just getting through. Some days I’m just doing what has to be done, and I don’t stop to ask myself if I got pleasure out of it. So I don’t want to say yes if that means something specific.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been feeling like myself, but whether that’s “down or hopeless most of the time” or “lost interest in most things,” I’m not sure I can separate it out that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, or found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know it probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m honestly not sure how to pin it down to “nearly every day.” I’ve felt… off. Heavy. Like there’s less room in me for anything good than there used to be. But I haven’t been marking the days in my head, like, “Today was hopeless, today wasn’t.”\n\nAnd with pleasure, I don’t know. There are things I used to do and I still do some of them, but sometimes it feels more like going through the motions than actually enjoying them. But then I worry I’m exaggerating if I say I “no longer” take pleasure in them, because I’m not sure that’s completely true either. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nSo… I guess the most honest answer is I don’t know if it’s nearly every day, and I don’t know if it’s no pleasure at all. I know I haven’t felt right, and I know things don’t land the way they used to. But I can’t give you a clean yes or no without feeling like I’m guessing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a clear answer, and I keep circling it. Almost every day… I don’t know. I really don’t know how to measure that honestly.\n\nI can say I’ve had a lot of heavy days. More than I used to. There are mornings where I wake up and it’s already kind of sitting there before anything even happens, like the day has this gray tone to it. But then I’ll get busy, or something needs doing, and I don’t stop and ask myself, “Am I hopeless right now?” So when you say almost every day, I start second-guessing whether I’m remembering it right or just lumping everything together because it all feels blurry.\n\nHopeless is a strong word too. Sometimes it feels like that, like I can’t see things getting lighter, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s been almost every single day for the last two weeks. I haven’t been tracking it that way. I know I haven’t felt okay. I know there’s been this low, dragged-down feeling around a lot. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time, or have you lost interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the time” is hard for me to judge, because I’m not walking around every minute thinking, “I’m depressed,” exactly. It’s more like I notice I’m heavier than I used to be, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if that counts the way you’re asking it.\n\nAnd interest… I mean, I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of what I enjoy versus what I’m just getting through. Some days I’m just doing what has to be done, and I don’t stop to ask myself if I got pleasure out of it. So I don’t want to say yes if that means something specific.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been feeling like myself, but whether that’s “down or hopeless most of the time” or “lost interest in most things,” I’m not sure I can separate it out that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, or found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know it probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m honestly not sure how to pin it down to “nearly every day.” I’ve felt… off. Heavy. Like there’s less room in me for anything good than there used to be. But I haven’t been marking the days in my head, like, “Today was hopeless, today wasn’t.”\n\nAnd with pleasure, I don’t know. There are things I used to do and I still do some of them, but sometimes it feels more like going through the motions than actually enjoying them. But then I worry I’m exaggerating if I say I “no longer” take pleasure in them, because I’m not sure that’s completely true either. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nSo… I guess the most honest answer is I don’t know if it’s nearly every day, and I don’t know if it’s no pleasure at all. I know I haven’t felt right, and I know things don’t land the way they used to. But I can’t give you a clean yes or no without feeling like I’m guessing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a clear answer, and I keep circling it. Almost every day… I don’t know. I really don’t know how to measure that honestly.\n\nI can say I’ve had a lot of heavy days. More than I used to. There are mornings where I wake up and it’s already kind of sitting there before anything even happens, like the day has this gray tone to it. But then I’ll get busy, or something needs doing, and I don’t stop and ask myself, “Am I hopeless right now?” So when you say almost every day, I start second-guessing whether I’m remembering it right or just lumping everything together because it all feels blurry.\n\nHopeless is a strong word too. Sometimes it feels like that, like I can’t see things getting lighter, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s been almost every single day for the last two weeks. I haven’t been tracking it that way. I know I haven’t felt okay. I know there’s been this low, dragged-down feeling around a lot. But if you need me to say yes or no to almost every day, I’m not sure I can say it cleanly without guessing.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, have you found yourself unable to enjoy things that used to bring you pleasure, almost every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can say “unable,” exactly. That sounds so absolute, like if something used to feel good, now it just gives me nothing every single time, and I don’t know if that’s true.

There are things I do where I think afterward, “I should have enjoyed that more,” or I realize I was sort of present physically but not really taking it in. But I haven’t been keeping a mental checklist of whether it happened almost every day. Sometimes I don’t even notice until later that I didn’t really feel much from it.

So… I guess I can say things haven’t been hitting the same way. Enjoyment feels harder to get to, maybe more muted. But if you’re asking whether I’ve been unable to enjoy things almost every day for the past two weeks, I don’t know that I can honestly give you a firm yes. I’m just not sure.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.9, F43.1, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is that I can’t get this thought out of my head. I’ve been an electrician for years, you know, and I always thought I was careful, competent, the kind of guy people could rely on. But if all that experience still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me?\n\nIt’s like… I keep thinking I’m dangerous. Not on purpose, not like I want to hurt anybody, but like there’s something wrong with me, like anyone who works with me or lives under my roof is taking a risk just being near me. And I know that probably sounds extreme, but it feels true in my chest. I can’t shake it. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe more about these thoughts? Are they something you actively try to suppress or ignore, or do they feel more like worries about real things that could happen?\n\nPatient: It feels less like a random weird thought I’m trying to push away and more like… like a conclusion my mind keeps coming back to, over and over. Like it’s evidence, you know? I keep thinking, I’ve spent years doing electrical work, learning the right way to do things, double-checking, being the guy who’s supposed to know better. And if even with all that, I couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what business do I have telling myself I’m careful?\n\nSo yeah, I do try to ignore it sometimes, because it’s exhausting, but it doesn’t feel like some nonsense thought I can just brush off. It feels like a real warning. Like I’m a danger to anybody who works with me, or even people living under my roof. Not because I mean to be, but because maybe I miss things, maybe I’m not as safe as I thought I was. And once that gets in my head, I start going through every possible way something could go wrong around me.\n\nI know part of me wants you to tell me that’s not reasonable, but sitting here saying it out loud… it still feels true. Like my experience should have been enough, and it wasn’t. So now I don’t trust myself.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you find yourself doing anything specific—like checking things over and over, counting, or going through a routine in your head—to try to make the anxiety go away or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think how to answer that without making it sound like something it isn’t.\n\nI mean, I’ll check things, sure, but I’m an electrician, so checking is kind of part of the job anyway. I don’t know that I do it in some exact ritual way, like counting a certain number of times or having a phrase I repeat in my head or anything like that. It’s more like I get stuck worrying that I missed something, and then I’ll go back over it in my mind, or I’ll look again, but I don’t know if that’s a “routine” exactly.\n\nAt home too, sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking through what could go wrong, but it’s not like there’s one specific thing I do that makes the anxiety go away. Honestly, it usually doesn’t go away. I might feel a little better for a second if I reassure myself, but then the same question comes back: what if I missed something?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’m counting or doing set rituals. I’m just kind of trapped in the checking-myself mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how much these thoughts upset you when they come up? Do they feel intrusive and unwanted, like they don’t really belong to you, or do they feel more like a natural part of your regular worries?\n\nPatient: They upset me a lot. I mean… not like a passing worry where you think, “Okay, I’m stressed, I’ll deal with it.” It hits me hard, and it stays. It’s like once that thought shows up, everything in me reacts to it like it’s true.\n\nAnd the thought is basically what I said when I came in — if all my years doing electrical work, all that training and experience and being careful, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then how am I supposed to believe I’m not a danger to other people? To somebody working next to me, or somebody living in the same house as me? It’s not just an abstract fear. It feels personal, like it says something about who I am.\n\nI don’t want the thought there. I hate it. But I also can’t say it feels like some random thing that doesn’t belong to me, because part of me believes it. That’s what makes it so hard. It feels like a natural worry in the sense that it comes from something real, but it’s also… it takes over. It’s like my mind keeps using it as proof that I can’t trust myself, and I can’t get enough distance from it to just say, “That’s ridiculous.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever lead you to take specific actions or make decisions—like avoiding certain tasks, withdrawing from others, or constantly seeking reassurance—to try to reduce the risk or the anxiety you feel?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I don’t know if I always think of it as “reducing anxiety” in the moment, because I’ll tell myself I’m just being practical, or I’m tired, or I don’t have time for something. But if I’m honest, yeah, I avoid things.\n\nThe biggest one is my garage. Which sounds stupid, because it’s my own house, you know? But I don’t go in there unless I absolutely have to. If I need a tool or something, I’ll kind of plan it out, go in, grab what I need, and get back out. I don’t just spend time out there anymore. I used to be able to work on things, mess around, organize stuff, whatever. Now it feels like as soon as I’m in there, my mind starts going places I don’t want it to go, and I just… I don’t want to be in that space.\n\nAnd I quit helping with the electrical stuff at church. I used to be the guy they’d ask—outlets, lights, little repairs, checking something that wasn’t working right. And I just stopped. I made excuses at first, like I was busy with work or I had too much going on, but really I don’t want that responsibility anymore. I don’t want to be the person someone trusts with something like that, because then if anything goes wrong, it’s on me. Even if it’s small, even if I know how to do it, I just don’t want to be in that position.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about car projects, or electrical work on cars, or anything around the fire, I shut it down pretty quick. I’ll change the subject or leave the room or say I don’t want to talk about it. Sometimes I probably come off harsh, but I can’t sit there and listen to people pick through it or talk casually about that kind of thing. It gets under my skin fast, and I feel like I have to stop it before it goes too far.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I do make decisions around it. I avoid places, I stopped doing things I used to do, and I keep people from talking about certain things around me. At the time it feels like I’m just trying to keep myself steady, but it’s definitely changed what I’ll let myself be around.\n\nDoctor: Are these kinds of thoughts something you've been dealing with even outside of situations related to your work or your brother's accident—like at other times when you're not actively working on electrical things or reminded of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only when I’m standing in front of a panel or holding a tool. That’s kind of the problem. It started from that, from my brother and from the work, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box.\n\nI can be at home doing something completely normal, and the thought is still there in the background — that if all my years as an electrician, all the training and habits and double-checking, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m dangerous just by being the person responsible for things. Not just on a job site, but under my own roof too.\n\nIt’ll come up when I’m around my family, or even just sitting in the house. I’ll think, “What if I miss something here? What if something I did, or didn’t do, hurts somebody?” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I can’t fully trust myself around people who depend on me. So yeah, it’s connected to the accident, but it follows me outside of the obvious reminders. It’s more like a belief I keep carrying around, not just a thought that shows up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having similar kinds of thoughts or worries about being responsible for harm in other areas of your life, not just related to electrical work or your brother’s accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it started with the electrical work and my brother, obviously, but it spread out from there. It’s not like my brain keeps it limited to, “Don’t touch wires” or “Don’t take on a job.” It’s more like… if I couldn’t keep him safe after all those years of knowing what I was doing, then what else am I missing?\n\nThat’s the thought I can’t shake. I came in because I can’t get away from this idea that if my training, my experience, all the caution I used to pride myself on, still wasn’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. And that’s not just about a circuit or a tool. That gets into everything. Like being around people, being responsible for them, having them trust me.\n\nI know that might sound broad, but that’s how it feels. It’s like the accident became proof in my head that I can’t trust my own judgment. So even in other parts of my life, the worry is still basically the same: what if someone gets hurt because I was the one there, the one responsible, the one who should’ve known better?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a bad or worthless person, even when you’re not thinking about the accident or your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not even like I have to be actively thinking about the accident for it to be there. It’s more like this… weight that just sits in me all day. Guilt, mostly, but not just guilt. Anger too. And then I feel guilty for being angry, so it kind of folds back in on itself.\n\nI don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” in those exact words all the time, but the feeling is there. Like I’m the person everybody got left with. Especially with my nephew. When I look at him, I can’t help thinking, he lost his dad and somehow he got stuck with me instead. And I know that sounds harsh, and I don’t want to put that on him — I don’t say that to him or anything — but inside, that’s where my mind goes. Like I’m this poor substitute. Like I’m not the one who should still be here trying to be useful.\n\nAnd it’s not just one quick thought and then it passes. It sits in my chest, this heavy, tight feeling, and I carry it into regular moments. Eating dinner, driving somewhere, seeing him do something normal. I’ll look at him and feel this mix of sadness and anger and shame, like I failed him before he even had a say in it. So yeah… even when I’m not actively replaying the accident or thinking about work, I still feel like there’s something bad about me. Like I’m not enough, and maybe I never will be.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time, or have you lost interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the time” is hard for me to judge, because I’m not walking around every minute thinking, “I’m depressed,” exactly. It’s more like I notice I’m heavier than I used to be, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if that counts the way you’re asking it.\n\nAnd interest… I mean, I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of what I enjoy versus what I’m just getting through. Some days I’m just doing what has to be done, and I don’t stop to ask myself if I got pleasure out of it. So I don’t want to say yes if that means something specific.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been feeling like myself, but whether that’s “down or hopeless most of the time” or “lost interest in most things,” I’m not sure I can separate it out that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, or found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know it probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m honestly not sure how to pin it down to “nearly every day.” I’ve felt… off. Heavy. Like there’s less room in me for anything good than there used to be. But I haven’t been marking the days in my head, like, “Today was hopeless, today wasn’t.”\n\nAnd with pleasure, I don’t know. There are things I used to do and I still do some of them, but sometimes it feels more like going through the motions than actually enjoying them. But then I worry I’m exaggerating if I say I “no longer” take pleasure in them, because I’m not sure that’s completely true either. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nSo… I guess the most honest answer is I don’t know if it’s nearly every day, and I don’t know if it’s no pleasure at all. I know I haven’t felt right, and I know things don’t land the way they used to. But I can’t give you a clean yes or no without feeling like I’m guessing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a clear answer, and I keep circling it. Almost every day… I don’t know. I really don’t know how to measure that honestly.\n\nI can say I’ve had a lot of heavy days. More than I used to. There are mornings where I wake up and it’s already kind of sitting there before anything even happens, like the day has this gray tone to it. I used to be the guy they’d ask—outlets, lights, little repairs, checking something that wasn’t working right. And I just stopped. I made excuses at first, like I was busy with work or I had too much going on, but really I don’t want that responsibility anymore. I don’t want to be the person someone trusts with something like that, because then if anything goes wrong, it’s on me. Even if it’s small, even if I know how to do it, I just don’t want to be in that position.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about car projects, or electrical work on cars, or anything around the fire, I shut it down pretty quick. I’ll change the subject or leave the room or say I don’t want to talk about it. Sometimes I probably come off harsh, but I can’t sit there and listen to people pick through it or talk casually about that kind of thing. It gets under my skin fast, and I feel like I have to stop it before it goes too far.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I do make decisions around it. I avoid places, I stopped doing things I used to do, and I keep people from talking about certain things around me. At the time it feels like I’m just trying to keep myself steady, but it’s definitely changed what I’ll let myself be around.\n\nDoctor: Are these kinds of thoughts something you've been dealing with even outside of situations related to your work or your brother's accident—like at other times when you're not actively working on electrical things or reminded of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only when I’m standing in front of a panel or holding a tool. That’s kind of the problem. It started from that, from my brother and from the work, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box.\n\nI can be at home doing something completely normal, and the thought is still there in the background — that if all my years as an electrician, all the training and habits and double-checking, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m dangerous just by being the person responsible for things. Not just on a job site, but under my own roof too.\n\nIt’ll come up when I’m around my family, or even just sitting in the house. I’ll think, “What if I miss something here? What if something I did, or didn’t do, hurts somebody?” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I can’t fully trust myself around people who depend on me. So yeah, it’s connected to the accident, but it follows me outside of the obvious reminders. It’s more like a belief I keep carrying around, not just a thought that shows up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having similar kinds of thoughts or worries about being responsible for harm in other areas of your life, not just related to electrical work or your brother’s accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it started with the electrical work and my brother, obviously, but it spread out from there. It’s not like my brain keeps it limited to, “Don’t touch wires” or “Don’t take on a job.” It’s more like… if I couldn’t keep him safe after all those years of knowing what I was doing, then what else am I missing?\n\nThat’s the thought I can’t shake. I came in because I can’t get away from this idea that if my training, my experience, all the caution I used to pride myself on, still wasn’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. And that’s not just about a circuit or a tool. That gets into everything. Like being around people, being responsible for them, having them trust me.\n\nI know that might sound broad, but that’s how it feels. It’s like the accident became proof in my head that I can’t trust my own judgment. So even in other parts of my life, the worry is still basically the same: what if someone gets hurt because I was the one there, the one responsible, the one who should’ve known better?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a bad or worthless person, even when you’re not thinking about the accident or your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not even like I have to be actively thinking about the accident for it to be there. It’s more like this… weight that just sits in me all day. Guilt, mostly, but not just guilt. Anger too. And then I feel guilty for being angry, so it kind of folds back in on itself.\n\nI don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” in those exact words all the time, but the feeling is there. Like I’m the person everybody got left with. Especially with my nephew. When I look at him, I can’t help thinking, he lost his dad and somehow he got stuck with me instead. And I know that sounds harsh, and I don’t want to put that on him — I don’t say that to him or anything — but inside, that’s where my mind goes. Like I’m this poor substitute. Like I’m not the one who should still be here trying to be useful.\n\nAnd it’s not just one quick thought and then it passes. It sits in my chest, this heavy, tight feeling, and I carry it into regular moments. Eating dinner, driving somewhere, seeing him do something normal. I’ll look at him and feel this mix of sadness and anger and shame, like I failed him before he even had a say in it. So yeah… even when I’m not actively replaying the accident or thinking about work, I still feel like there’s something bad about me. Like I’m not enough, and maybe I never will be.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time, or have you lost interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the time” is hard for me to judge, because I’m not walking around every minute thinking, “I’m depressed,” exactly. It’s more like I notice I’m heavier than I used to be, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if that counts the way you’re asking it.\n\nAnd interest… I mean, I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of what I enjoy versus what I’m just getting through. Some days I’m just doing what has to be done, and I don’t stop to ask myself if I got pleasure out of it. So I don’t want to say yes if that means something specific.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been feeling like myself, but whether that’s “down or hopeless most of the time” or “lost interest in most things,” I’m not sure I can separate it out that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, or found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know it probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m honestly not sure how to pin it down to “nearly every day.” I’ve felt… off. Heavy. Like there’s less room in me for anything good than there used to be. But I haven’t been marking the days in my head, like, “Today was hopeless, today wasn’t.”\n\nAnd with pleasure, I don’t know. There are things I used to do and I still do some of them, but sometimes it feels more like going through the motions than actually enjoying them. But then I worry I’m exaggerating if I say I “no longer” take pleasure in them, because I’m not sure that’s completely true either. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nSo… I guess the most honest answer is I don’t know if it’s nearly every day, and I don’t know if it’s no pleasure at all. I know I haven’t felt right, and I know things don’t land the way they used to. But I can’t give you a clean yes or no without feeling like I’m guessing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a clear answer, and I keep circling it. Almost every day… I don’t know. I really don’t know how to measure that honestly.\n\nI can say I’ve had a lot of heavy days. More than I used to. There are mornings where I wake up and it’s already kind of sitting there before anything even happens, like the day has this gray tone to it. But then I’ll get busy, or something needs doing, and I don’t stop and ask myself, “Am I hopeless right now?” So when you say almost every day, I start second-guessing whether I’m remembering it right or just lumping everything together because it all feels blurry.\n\nHopeless is a strong word too. Sometimes it feels like that, like I can’t see things getting lighter, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s been almost every single day for the last two weeks. I haven’t been tracking it that way. I know I haven’t felt okay. I know there’s been this low, dragged-down feeling around a lot. But if you need me to say yes or no to almost every day, I’m not sure I can say it cleanly without guessing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself unable to enjoy things that used to bring you pleasure, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “unable,” exactly. That sounds so absolute, like if something used to feel good, now it just gives me nothing every single time, and I don’t know if that’s true.\n\nThere are things I do where I think afterward, “I should have enjoyed that more,” or I realize I was sort of present physically but not really taking it in. But I haven’t been keeping a mental checklist of whether it happened almost every day. Sometimes I don’t even notice until later that I didn’t really feel much from it.\n\nSo… I guess I can say things haven’t been hitting the same way. Enjoyment feels harder to get to, maybe more muted. But if you’re asking whether I’ve been unable to enjoy things almost every day for the past two weeks, I don’t know that I can honestly give you a firm yes. I’m just not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.1, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is that I can’t get this thought out of my head. I’ve been an electrician for years, you know, and I always thought I was careful, competent, the kind of guy people could rely on. But if all that experience still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me?\n\nIt’s like… I keep thinking I’m dangerous. Not on purpose, not like I want to hurt anybody, but like there’s something wrong with me, like anyone who works with me or lives under my roof is taking a risk just being near me. Are they something you actively try to suppress or ignore, or do they feel more like worries about real things that could happen?\n\nPatient: It feels less like a random weird thought I’m trying to push away and more like… like a conclusion my mind keeps coming back to, over and over. Like it’s evidence, you know? I keep thinking, I’ve spent years doing electrical work, learning the right way to do things, double-checking, being the guy who’s supposed to know better. And if even with all that, I couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what business do I have telling myself I’m careful?\n\nSo yeah, I do try to ignore it sometimes, because it’s exhausting, but it doesn’t feel like some nonsense thought I can just brush off. It feels like a real warning. Like I’m a danger to anybody who works with me, or even people living under my roof. Not because I mean to be, but because maybe I miss things, maybe I’m not as safe as I thought I was. And once that gets in my head, I start going through every possible way something could go wrong around me.\n\nI know part of me wants you to tell me that’s not reasonable, but sitting here saying it out loud… it still feels true. Like my experience should have been enough, and it wasn’t. So now I don’t trust myself.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you find yourself doing anything specific—like checking things over and over, counting, or going through a routine in your head—to try to make the anxiety go away or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think how to answer that without making it sound like something it isn’t.\n\nI mean, I’ll check things, sure, but I’m an electrician, so checking is kind of part of the job anyway. I don’t know that I do it in some exact ritual way, like counting a certain number of times or having a phrase I repeat in my head or anything like that. It’s more like I get stuck worrying that I missed something, and then I’ll go back over it in my mind, or I’ll look again, but I don’t know if that’s a “routine” exactly.\n\nAt home too, sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking through what could go wrong, but it’s not like there’s one specific thing I do that makes the anxiety go away. Honestly, it usually doesn’t go away. I might feel a little better for a second if I reassure myself, but then the same question comes back: what if I missed something?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’m counting or doing set rituals. I’m just kind of trapped in the checking-myself mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how much these thoughts upset you when they come up? Do they feel intrusive and unwanted, like they don’t really belong to you, or do they feel more like a natural part of your regular worries?\n\nPatient: They upset me a lot. I mean… not like a passing worry where you think, “Okay, I’m stressed, I’ll deal with it.” It hits me hard, and it stays. It’s like once that thought shows up, everything in me reacts to it like it’s true.\n\nAnd the thought is basically what I said when I came in — if all my years doing electrical work, all that training and experience and being careful, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then how am I supposed to believe I’m not a danger to other people? To somebody working next to me, or somebody living in the same house as me? It’s not just an abstract fear. It feels personal, like it says something about who I am.\n\nI don’t want the thought there. I hate it. But I also can’t say it feels like some random thing that doesn’t belong to me, because part of me believes it. That’s what makes it so hard. It feels like a natural worry in the sense that it comes from something real, but it’s also… it takes over. It’s like my mind keeps using it as proof that I can’t trust myself, and I can’t get enough distance from it to just say, “That’s ridiculous.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever lead you to take specific actions or make decisions—like avoiding certain tasks, withdrawing from others, or constantly seeking reassurance—to try to reduce the risk or the anxiety you feel?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I don’t know if I always think of it as “reducing anxiety” in the moment, because I’ll tell myself I’m just being practical, or I’m tired, or I don’t have time for something. But if I’m honest, yeah, I avoid things.\n\nThe biggest one is my garage. Which sounds stupid, because it’s my own house, you know? But I don’t go in there unless I absolutely have to. If I need a tool or something, I’ll kind of plan it out, go in, grab what I need, and get back out. I don’t just spend time out there anymore. I used to be able to work on things, mess around, organize stuff, whatever. Now it feels like as soon as I’m in there, my mind starts going places I don’t want it to go, and I just… I don’t want to be in that space.\n\nAnd I quit helping with the electrical stuff at church. I used to be the guy they’d ask—outlets, lights, little repairs, checking something that wasn’t working right. And I just stopped. I made excuses at first, like I was busy with work or I had too much going on, but really I don’t want that responsibility anymore. I don’t want to be the person someone trusts with something like that, because then if anything goes wrong, it’s on me. Even if it’s small, even if I know how to do it, I just don’t want to be in that position.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about car projects, or electrical work on cars, or anything around the fire, I shut it down pretty quick. I’ll change the subject or leave the room or say I don’t want to talk about it. Sometimes I probably come off harsh, but I can’t sit there and listen to people pick through it or talk casually about that kind of thing. It gets under my skin fast, and I feel like I have to stop it before it goes too far.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I do make decisions around it. I avoid places, I stopped doing things I used to do, and I keep people from talking about certain things around me. At the time it feels like I’m just trying to keep myself steady, but it’s definitely changed what I’ll let myself be around.\n\nDoctor: Are these kinds of thoughts something you've been dealing with even outside of situations related to your work or your brother's accident—like at other times when you're not actively working on electrical things or reminded of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only when I’m standing in front of a panel or holding a tool. That’s kind of the problem. It started from that, from my brother and from the work, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box.\n\nI can be at home doing something completely normal, and the thought is still there in the background — that if all my years as an electrician, all the training and habits and double-checking, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m dangerous just by being the person responsible for things. Not just on a job site, but under my own roof too.\n\nIt’ll come up when I’m around my family, or even just sitting in the house. I’ll think, “What if I miss something here? What if something I did, or didn’t do, hurts somebody?” And then it turns into this bigger thing, like I can’t fully trust myself around people who depend on me. So yeah, it’s connected to the accident, but it follows me outside of the obvious reminders. It’s more like a belief I keep carrying around, not just a thought that shows up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having similar kinds of thoughts or worries about being responsible for harm in other areas of your life, not just related to electrical work or your brother’s accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it started with the electrical work and my brother, obviously, but it spread out from there. It’s not like my brain keeps it limited to, “Don’t touch wires” or “Don’t take on a job.” It’s more like… if I couldn’t keep him safe after all those years of knowing what I was doing, then what else am I missing?\n\nThat’s the thought I can’t shake. I came in because I can’t get away from this idea that if my training, my experience, all the caution I used to pride myself on, still wasn’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. And that’s not just about a circuit or a tool. That gets into everything. Like being around people, being responsible for them, having them trust me.\n\nI know that might sound broad, but that’s how it feels. It’s like the accident became proof in my head that I can’t trust my own judgment. So even in other parts of my life, the worry is still basically the same: what if someone gets hurt because I was the one there, the one responsible, the one who should’ve known better?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a bad or worthless person, even when you’re not thinking about the accident or your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not even like I have to be actively thinking about the accident for it to be there. It’s more like this… weight that just sits in me all day. Guilt, mostly, but not just guilt. Anger too. And then I feel guilty for being angry, so it kind of folds back in on itself.\n\nI don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” in those exact words all the time, but the feeling is there. Like I’m the person everybody got left with. Especially with my nephew. When I look at him, I can’t help thinking, he lost his dad and somehow he got stuck with me instead. And I know that sounds harsh, and I don’t want to put that on him — I don’t say that to him or anything — but inside, that’s where my mind goes. Like I’m this poor substitute. Like I’m not the one who should still be here trying to be useful.\n\nAnd it’s not just one quick thought and then it passes. It sits in my chest, this heavy, tight feeling, and I carry it into regular moments. Eating dinner, driving somewhere, seeing him do something normal. I’ll look at him and feel this mix of sadness and anger and shame, like I failed him before he even had a say in it. So yeah… even when I’m not actively replaying the accident or thinking about work, I still feel like there’s something bad about me. Like I’m not enough, and maybe I never will be.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time, or have you lost interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the time” is hard for me to judge, because I’m not walking around every minute thinking, “I’m depressed,” exactly. It’s more like I notice I’m heavier than I used to be, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if that counts the way you’re asking it.\n\nAnd interest… I mean, I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of what I enjoy versus what I’m just getting through. Some days I’m just doing what has to be done, and I don’t stop to ask myself if I got pleasure out of it. So I don’t want to say yes if that means something specific.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been feeling like myself, but whether that’s “down or hopeless most of the time” or “lost interest in most things,” I’m not sure I can separate it out that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, or found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know it probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m honestly not sure how to pin it down to “nearly every day.” I’ve felt… off. Heavy. Like there’s less room in me for anything good than there used to be. But I haven’t been marking the days in my head, like, “Today was hopeless, today wasn’t.”\n\nAnd with pleasure, I don’t know. There are things I used to do and I still do some of them, but sometimes it feels more like going through the motions than actually enjoying them. But then I worry I’m exaggerating if I say I “no longer” take pleasure in them, because I’m not sure that’s completely true either. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nSo… I guess the most honest answer is I don’t know if it’s nearly every day, and I don’t know if it’s no pleasure at all. I know I haven’t felt right, and I know things don’t land the way they used to. But I can’t give you a clean yes or no without feeling like I’m guessing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a clear answer, and I keep circling it. Almost every day… I don’t know. I really don’t know how to measure that honestly.\n\nI can say I’ve had a lot of heavy days. More than I used to. There are mornings where I wake up and it’s already kind of sitting there before anything even happens, like the day has this gray tone to it. But then I’ll get busy, or something needs doing, and I don’t stop and ask myself, “Am I hopeless right now?” So when you say almost every day, I start second-guessing whether I’m remembering it right or just lumping everything together because it all feels blurry.\n\nHopeless is a strong word too. Sometimes it feels like that, like I can’t see things getting lighter, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s been almost every single day for the last two weeks. I haven’t been tracking it that way. I know I haven’t felt okay. I know there’s been this low, dragged-down feeling around a lot. But if you need me to say yes or no to almost every day, I’m not sure I can say it cleanly without guessing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself unable to enjoy things that used to bring you pleasure, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “unable,” exactly. That sounds so absolute, like if something used to feel good, now it just gives me nothing every single time, and I don’t know if that’s true.\n\nThere are things I do where I think afterward, “I should have enjoyed that more,” or I realize I was sort of present physically but not really taking it in. But I haven’t been keeping a mental checklist of whether it happened almost every day. Sometimes I don’t even notice until later that I didn’t really feel much from it.\n\nSo… I guess I can say things haven’t been hitting the same way. Enjoyment feels harder to get to, maybe more muted. Some days I’m just moving through what I have to do, and I’m not really stopping to label how I feel hour by hour.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say no like everything’s fine, because it isn’t. Yeah… I mean, when you put it that way, I think maybe the answer is closer to yes than I was saying before, but it doesn’t feel like just “sad” or “depressed” in a simple way.

It’s more like this heavy mix of guilt and anger sitting in my chest most of the day. It’s there when I’m trying to work, it’s there when I’m making dinner, it’s there when things get quiet. And especially when I look at my nephew, it just… it hits hard. I think about how he lost his dad, and then somehow he got stuck with me instead, and I don’t know how to explain that without sounding awful. I get angry that it happened, and then I feel guilty for being angry, and then guilty that I’m not better for him.

So if you’re asking whether I’ve felt down most of the day nearly every day, I guess I’d say there’s been this heavy thing there almost all the time. It’s not always me crying or lying in bed or something obvious like that. Sometimes I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do. But inside, yeah, it’s been pretty constant.

I mean, I don’t know if I always think of it as “reducing anxiety” in the moment, because I’ll tell myself I’m just being practical, or I’m tired, or I don’t have time for something. But if I’m honest, yeah, I avoid things.\n\nThe biggest one is my garage. Which sounds stupid, because it’s my own house, you know? But I don’t go in there unless I absolutely have to. If I need a tool or something, I’ll kind of plan it out, go in, grab what I need, and get back out. I don’t just spend time out there anymore. I used to be able to work on things, mess around, organize stuff, whatever. I don’t want to be the person someone trusts with something like that, because then if anything goes wrong, it’s on me. Even if it’s small, even if I know how to do it, I just don’t want to be in that position.\n\nAnd conversations too. I’ll change the subject or leave the room or say I don’t want to talk about it. That’s kind of the problem. It started from that, from my brother and from the work, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box.\n\nI can be at home doing something completely normal, and the thought is still there in the background — that if all my years as an electrician, all the training and habits and double-checking, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? I mean, it started with the electrical work and my brother, obviously, but it spread out from there. It’s not like my brain keeps it limited to, “Don’t touch wires” or “Don’t take on a job.” It’s more like… if I couldn’t keep him safe after all those years of knowing what I was doing, then what else am I missing?\n\nThat’s the thought I can’t shake. I came in because I can’t get away from this idea that if my training, my experience, all the caution I used to pride myself on, still wasn’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. And that’s not just about a circuit or a tool. That gets into everything. Like being around people, being responsible for them, having them trust me.\n\nI know that might sound broad, but that’s how it feels. It’s like the accident became proof in my head that I can’t trust my own judgment. So even in other parts of my life, the worry is still basically the same: what if someone gets hurt because I was the one there, the one responsible, the one who should’ve known better?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re a bad or worthless person, even when you’re not thinking about the accident or your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not even like I have to be actively thinking about the accident for it to be there. It’s more like this… weight that just sits in me all day. Guilt, mostly, but not just guilt. Anger too. And then I feel guilty for being angry, so it kind of folds back in on itself.\n\nI don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” in those exact words all the time, but the feeling is there. Like I’m the person everybody got left with. Especially with my nephew. When I look at him, I can’t help thinking, he lost his dad and somehow he got stuck with me instead. And I know that sounds harsh, and I don’t want to put that on him — I don’t say that to him or anything — but inside, that’s where my mind goes. Like I’m this poor substitute. Like I’m not the one who should still be here trying to be useful.\n\nAnd it’s not just one quick thought and then it passes. It sits in my chest, this heavy, tight feeling, and I carry it into regular moments. Eating dinner, driving somewhere, seeing him do something normal. So yeah… even when I’m not actively replaying the accident or thinking about work, I still feel like there’s something bad about me. Like I’m not enough, and maybe I never will be.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time, or have you lost interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the time” is hard for me to judge, because I’m not walking around every minute thinking, “I’m depressed,” exactly. It’s more like I notice I’m heavier than I used to be, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if that counts the way you’re asking it.\n\nAnd interest… I mean, I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of what I enjoy versus what I’m just getting through. Some days I’m just doing what has to be done, and I don’t stop to ask myself if I got pleasure out of it. So I don’t want to say yes if that means something specific.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been feeling like myself, but whether that’s “down or hopeless most of the time” or “lost interest in most things,” I’m not sure I can separate it out that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, or found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know it probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m honestly not sure how to pin it down to “nearly every day.” I’ve felt… off. Heavy. Like there’s less room in me for anything good than there used to be. But I haven’t been marking the days in my head, like, “Today was hopeless, today wasn’t.”\n\nAnd with pleasure, I don’t know.
